\maketitle
\begin{abstract}
Training multilingual language models requires makingrepeated decisions abouta variety of training settings, such as data mixtures and architectures,that are typically guided by results from ablations at smallerevaluations of small proxy scales. However, such resultsmodels.
Such evaluations are useful, however, onlyuseful if evaluations ofresults from small-scale models reliably predict ablation effects infollow the same trends as their larger-scale models.counterparts. 
While the conditions under which small-scale evaluations are predictive have been studied extensively in monolingual settings, these findings do not necessarily transfer to multilingual models,training, where model capacity is shared across languages,languages and scaling behavior dependscan depend strongly on thelanguage composition of the training mixture. 
In%a challenge compounded in multilingual settings where many language–specific tasks exhibit little measurable signal at small scales.
This work asks four foundational questions around this work,practice: (1) for multilingual language models, at what model size do evaluations become sufficiently predictive of larger models? (2) how early during pretraining can decisions be taken? (3) can we determine before doing many expensive evaluations whether a particular evaluation is likely to be informative at small-scale? and (4) how do these patterns change as multilingual training data varies in composition? 
We pretrain a controlled ladder of \nmodelsmultilingual models spanning six sizes from 90M to 1.7B non-embedding parameters and six language settings from 1 to 50 languages, with four design interventions and seed replicates, providing a testbed for the reliability of evaluations as effective predictors of scaling behavior. 

We evaluate multiple checkpoints of models in our scaling ladder using per-language bits per byte over 100 languages, as well as tasks from \nbenchmarks multilingual benchmark families. Our results characterize which benchmark families exhibit predictable scaling across model size and training. %, distinguishing them from benchmarks whose scaling is weak, noisy, or non-monotonic. 
For each benchmark, we also quantify how well the performance of smaller models predicts larger model performance, and how early in training this correspondence emerges. We then test whether these small-scale evaluations recover the relative effects of our data-mixture and architecture interventions observed at full scale. Finally, we ask whether the reliability of a small-scale evaluation can itself be anticipated cheaply, comparing measurements that assess whether a benchmark has sufficient signal to recover full-scale results across model scales and as a function of the number and composition of training languages. We release the ladder and the per-checkpoint measurements to support the identification of evaluations that can lead to cheaper, better-informed multilingual model development.

% %replicates. 
We evaluate 12 aligned checkpoints from each run using per-language bits per byte over 100 languages and tasks from \nbenchmarks multilingual benchmark families.
%% RQ1%
We characterize which benchmark families exhibit predictable scaling across model size and training, and distinguish benchmarks whose performance improves consistently from those whose scaling is weak, noisy, or non-monotonic. 
%% RQ2%
For each benchmark, we quantify how well performance at smaller model sizes predicts performance at larger sizes, and how early in training this correspondence emerges. We then test whether these small-scale evaluations recover the relative effects of our data-mixture and architecture interventions observed at full scale.
%% RQ3%
Finally, we ask whether the reliability of a small-scale evaluation can itself be anticipated cheaply, comparing measurements that assess whether a benchmark has sufficient signal to recover full-scale results across model scales and as a function of the number and composition of training languages.
%% RQ4
%% Finally, we test whether scaling behavior learned from observed languages transfers to held-out languages, and how this transfer depends on the underlying design intervention. 
%% We find that likelihood-based measurements scale predictably while four-option knowledge benchmarks sit at chance up to 1.7B; that a 350M proxy at 20\% of its run already reads a data decision the way the 1.7B reference does on bits per byte, while the benchmark suite reads every decision as a coin flip; that the reliability of a proxy is set by the size of the intervention's effect relative to seed noise rather than by the intervention itself; and that scaling exponents transfer across languages, so that one small measurement predicts a never-trained language's bits per byte at 1.7B within 7\%.% 
We release the ladder and the per-checkpoint measurements to support cheaper, better-informed multilingual model development.
\end{abstract}

\section{Introduction}
\label{sec:introduction}

Modern language model development increasingly relies on smaller proxy models and intermediate training checkpoints to guide decisions about data mixtures, data sampling strategies, architectures, tokenizers, and other training choices.
For the results of these smaller training runs to guide training at scale, they must lead to making the same decisions as if the run had been performed with a fully-trained larger model.
This predictability has been previously studied in the monolingual setting \citep{magnusson_datadecide_2025}, where small proxy models provide good signal about which design choices are best for larger ones. Indeed, inability to use proxy models or earlier checkpoints in the monolingual setting  is typically attributed to failures of the specific benchmark rather than lack of signal from the proxy runs
\citep{heineman_signal_2025}.

Multilingual language model development introduces a host of factors that could cause proxy run results to become noisy. %, such as cross-language transfer and interference and undertraining of languages with smaller portions of the token budget.
Languages vary in data availability, tokenization cost, and scaling behavior \citep{petrov_language_2023, ahia_all_2023,mueninghof-data-constrained}, while cross-lingual transfer further couples decisions across languages, meaning that adding data for one language can help or hurt others depending on model capacity and language similarity \citep{chang_when_2023, longpre_atlas_2026}. 
The token budget of a proxy run is also divided among its languages: while a total token budget may look large, languages receiving small fractions of the share can easily be undertrained. 
%: in the settings we study, half of each budget is English and the remainder is divided among up to 49 languages in proportion to corpus size, and a 350M-parameter proxy trained on 50 languages gives a language holding 0.5\% of the non-English half about 86M training tokens. 
As a result, a benchmark that provides a reliable training signal for one language or scale may be uninformative for another, making the use of proxy runs for% RQ1: For multilingual language model development questionable.%\CL{whether an evaluation of a language is usablemodels, at a proxy point depends on the training tokens of that language, a quantity thatwhat model size alone does not determine}.do evaluations become sufficiently predictive of larger models? 

We see empirical evidence of this already: many multilingual language--benchmark pairs are known to exhibit little measurable signal at small scales, with performance often remaining near chance or floor levels.
In our own sweep, task accuracy exceeds the chance level in 4.0\% of Belebele's language--size cells over the five sizes from 175M to 1.7B non-embedding parameters, in 2.2\% for Global-MMLU, and in 79.4\% for HellaSwag. A comparison read from a score at chance selects% RQ2: How early during pretraining can you evaluate a candidate by noise, potentially leading to suboptimal decisionsmodel and still reliably identify the effects that would be observed at the full costend of all these proxy runs.training?

We organize this study as three questions, in% RQ3: Can we determine before doing the orderexpensive evaluation whether a developer asks them.
First,particular small-scale evaluation is likely to be informative? In other words, are there cheap measurements that tell you whether a benchmark evaluated at which training-token countsa small size has enough signal to support decisions?

% RQ4: How do all of these relationships change as the training mixture changes, from monolingual to increasingly multilingual settings, including differences in language coverage and/or balance?

Modern language model development increasingly relies on smaller proxy models and intermediate training checkpoints to guide decisions about data mixtures, data sampling strategies, architectures, tokenizers, and other training choices. 
%and are typically evaluated with benchmark suites that differ substantially in their diagnostic value and variance. 
For the results of these smaller training runs to guide training at scale, they must lead to making the same decisions as if the run had been performed with a fully-trained larger model. This predictability has been previously studied in the monolingual setting \citep{magnusson_datadecide_2025}, but multilingual language does an evaluation producemodel development introduces a score distinguishable from its own noise (\S\ref{subsec:rq0})? For accuracy tasks, we applyhost of other factors that could cause proxy model results to become noisy, such as cross-language transfer and interference, and an expansive space of possible data mixtures as the number of languages grows. 
% We see empirical evidence of this already: many multilingual language–benchmark are known to exhibit little measurable signal at small scales, with performance often remaining near chance test above;or floor levels. 
% These factors make the language dimension a substantial expansion of the experimental space.

Multilingual scaling laws provide one approach for per-language bits per byte, which has no chance level, we test whetherpredicting the scorescaling behavior of LLMs, by modeling how performance changesconsistently with model sizesize, data allocation, and withlanguage mixture, and by accounting for cross-language transfer \citep{he2025scaling}. 
However, scaling laws focus primarily on training tokens.
Second, among the evaluations that pass this test, at which proxy sizesloss and likelihood-based objectives, where scaling behavior is often smoother and measurable earlier in training. In contrast, many training fractions doesablations are run to predict the rankingeffect of model variants ator data decision on more complex downstream behaviors (such as knowledge retention or commonsense reasoning), which is typically measured by performance on benchmarks. Consequently, providing useful insights for scaling behavior  requires informative benchmarks that can (a) robustly disentangle the proxy point agree with their rankingperformance effects of training decisions when evaluating small-scale models, and (b) encourage decisions at small scale that would have the reference point,same effect on larger-scale models.

% why multilingual evaluation makes decision reliability harder.
%Consequently, providing useful insights for the fully trained largest model (\S\ref{subsec:rq2})?
Third, which statistics computed fromscaling behavior of multilingual models requires informative benchmarks that can robustly disentangle the proxy runs alone predictperformance effects of all these possible training decisions at small scale. 
Recent work has begun to examine the diagnostic value and efficiency of language model benchmarks, recognizing that not all benchmarks provide equally informative signals for guiding model development.
Benchmark-discrimination analyses such as SNR \citep{heineman_signal_2025}, MetaEval \citep{wang2026metaeval} and \citet{chen_scaling_2025, gupta_improving_2025, zhou_lost_2026} characterize whether an evaluation contains enough reliable signal, and have largely been developed in settings where evaluation signal is already measurable. However, it is unclear whether their conclusions transfer to multilingual model development, where downstream performance can remain near floor at small scales and ranking agreement may differ substantially across languages. 
Languages vary in data availability, tokenization cost, and scaling behavior \citep{petrov_language_2023, ahia_all_2023}, while cross-lingual transfer further couples decisions across languages, meaning that agreement, soadding data for one language can help or hurt others depending on model capacity and language similarity \citep{chang_when_2023, longpre_atlas_2026}. As a result, a benchmark that provides a developer can assessreliable training signal for one language or scale may be uninformative for another.

This makes it important to understand when downstream benchmark performance becomes predictable, when the decisions it supports stabilize across scale, and whether cheap measurements can tell us when to trust them.

\section{Experimental Setup}
\label{sec:methodology}
We study the problem setting in which a developer chooses between candidate settings of one design choice in language model pretraining: for example, between a deep and a shallow architecture, between two rules for selecting the training languages, or between two sampling temperatures for the training mixture. The outcome of interest is the evaluation score of a fully trained model at a target size, so the correct choice is the candidate whose fully trained model at that size has the best score. Training a model at the target size for every candidate is too expensive, so the developer instead trains the candidates at a cheaper \emph{proxy point}, a smaller model size, an earlier checkpoint of the same size, or both, and selects the candidate with the best score there. The selection at the proxy point is correct when the candidate selected there is also the candidate with the best score among the fully trained models at the target size. This paper measures when the selection at the proxy point is correct, as a function of the evaluation, the proxy point, and the number of training tokens of the evaluated language that the proxy checkpoint was trained on.

Stating this precisely requires notationConsider a model trained with non-embedding parameter count $N$, architecture $A$, number of training languages $K$, language-selection scheme $M$ (\ie{} the rule for whatchoosing the compared training runs sharepretraining languages), and where they differ. Adata sampling temperature $T$ (\ie{} the way of sampling across languages).
We define \emph{model configuration} as
\[
\theta=(N,A,K,M,T)\theta=(N,A,K,M,T).
\]
records the design choices of one training run: the non-embedding parameter count $N$, the architecture $A$, the number of training languages $K$, the language-selection scheme $M$ (\ie{} the rule for choosing the pretraining languages), and the data sampling temperature $T$ (\ie{} the rule for allocating training tokens across languages).
These five components are the intervention axes of \S\ref{subsec:models}. The candidates of one design choice are configurations that differ in exactly one component; we call them \emph{model variants}. Comparisons between configurations that differ in more than one component are defined in \S\ref{subsec:methodology-da}.
For each configuration, we train one or more runs with random seed $S$ and save checkpoints at training fractions $f\in (0,1.0]$.
Our evaluation suite contains benchmarks $b$, each comprising language tasks $b_\ell$. For example, the HellaSwag benchmark contains tasks such as HellaSwag-es, HellaSwag-hi, and HellaSwag-gr. Evaluating checkpoint $(\theta,S,f)$ on task $b_\ell$ produces the score
$Y_{b_\ell}(\theta,S,f)$.
We use these scores to test whether rankings obtained at smaller sizes or earlier training fractions agree with rankings at a reference point.


We first describe the model ladder (\S\ref{subsec:models}) and evaluation protocol (\S\ref{subsec:evaluation}), we then define ranking agreement (RA) across model size and training progress (\S\ref{subsec:methodology-da}). We finally introduce statistics that may predict this agreement without evaluating the target models (\S\ref{subsec:methodology-snr}).

Measurements without a meaningful chance level, such as bits per byte, are not filtered out by this procedure.
We report the implementation and statistics of the gate in Appendix~\ref{app:chance-threshold}.

\subsection{Ranking Agreement}
\label{subsec:methodology-da}

Ranking agreement (RA) measures whether a set of model variants is ranked in the same order at a cheaper \emph{proxy point} and at the  \emph{reference point}. 
A proxy point is defined by a model size and training fraction $(N,f)$, while the reference point is the final checkpoint ($f=1$) at the same size $N$ or at the largestreference size shared by all$N_{\mathrm{ref}}=$ 1.7B, which every compared variants, $N_{\mathrm{ref}}$.variant reaches.
RA ranges from 0 to 1, where 1 indicates perfect agreement with the reference ranking and 0 indicates no agreement.
Therefore, higher RA means that evaluation at the proxy point is more likely to support the same development decision as the full reference evaluation.
We consider the three transfer settings defined in Table~\ref{tab:ra_defs}.


We quantify ranking agreement using three complementary metrics. Spearman's rank correlation $\rho$ measures agreement between the complete proxy and reference rankings.
Kendall's $\tau_a = (C-D)/n$ counts the concordant ($C$) minus the discordant ($D$) pairs among the $n$ pairs of variants.
Decision accuracy (DA;(DA, \citealp{heineman_signal_2025}) measures the fraction of pairwise preferences that agree between them.
%For a set $\mathcal{P}$ of model-variant pairs,
%\begin{equation}
%\mathrm{DA} = \frac{1}{|\mathcal{P}|} \sum_{(a,b)\in\mathcal{P}} \mathbb{1}
%\left[ \operatorname{sign}\!\left(U(s_a)-U(s_b)\right) =
%\operatorname{sign}\!\left(U(m_a)-U(m_b)\right) \right],
%\label{eq:decision-accuracy}
%\end{equation}
%where $s_a$ and $m_a$ denote variant $a$ at the small proxy and reference points, respectively, and $U(\cdot)$ returns the benchmark score.
For a language task $b_\ell$, a proxy point $(N,f)$ and a set $\mathcal{P}$ of pairs of model configurations,
\begin{equation}
\mathrm{DA}_{b_\ell}(N,f)
=
\frac{1}{|\mathcal{P}|}
\sum_{(\theta_a,\theta_b)\in\mathcal{P}}
\mathbb{1}
\left[
\operatorname{sign}\!\left(Y_{b_\ell}(\theta_a,f)-Y_{b_\ell}(\theta_b,f)\right)
=
\operatorname{sign}\!\left(Y_{b_\ell}(\theta_a^{\mathrm{ref}},1)-Y_{b_\ell}(\theta_b^{\mathrm{ref}},1)\right)
\right],
\label{eq:decision-accuracy}
\end{equation}
where $\theta_a$ has size $N$, $\theta_a^{\mathrm{ref}}$ is the same configuration at $N_{\mathrm{ref}}$. A pair tied on both sides counts as agreement and a pair tied on one side as disagreement. DA equals one when every pairwise preference transfers and equals $0.5$ in expectation under random, tie-free rankings. DA and Kendall's $\tau$ differ only in how they treat ties: $2\,\mathrm{DA}-1 = \tau_a + (T_{\mathrm{both}}-T_{\mathrm{one}})/n$, where $T_{\mathrm{both}}$ and $T_{\mathrm{one}}$ count the pairs tied on both sides and on one side. A cell task--size--checkpoint$(b_\ell, N, f)$ is reported if it has at least three pairs.
We summarize a benchmark or a suite by the mean of the cell values over its gated tasks. Where we restrict to reliably ranking tasks, we keep those whose median DA over the proxy cells reaches 0.66 for RA-size or for RA-checkpoint. Formal definitions can be found in Appendix~\ref{app:ranking-agreement}.Such a reading is conditional on the selection.


\textbf{Ranking comparison axes.}
When computing RA, we distinguish between two types of model comparisons based on which design choices vary.
Pairs are formed among the seed-1904 runs of every architecture and data build. The replicate seeds serve only as a noise baseline and are never ranked as design variants.
In \emph{single-axis} comparisons, models differ in only one model design choice ($K$, depth, activation, $M$ or $T$) while all others are held fixed. 
For example, we compare deep and shallow architectures ($A$), while keeping the language count, language scheme, and sampling temperature unchanged
($\theta' = (A_\mathrm{deep},K,M,T) \rightarrow \theta'' = (A_\mathrm{shallow},K,M,T)$).
This setting reflects a typical controlled development ablation where we test whether a cheaper proxy leads to the same choice between two alternatives as the reference evaluation.

To isolate this effect, we reformulate the three benchmarks of Belebele, Global-MMLU, and INCLUDE so that the model scores the full answer options directly, rather than predicting their corresponding letter labels.
We apply two reformulations. The first is programmatic: we remove the answer labels and score the four options directly, leaving the text of every item unchanged.
The second is model-based: we prompt \texttt{gemini-3.8-flash} \citep{gemini_team_gemini_2024} to restate each question as a declarative stem and each option as a statement completing it, without revealing which option is correct.
Both reformulations substantially increase the number of benchmark--languagebenchmark-language subsets that pass the above-chance filter at 1.7B. The programmatic one raises it from 5 to 86 of 105 for Belebele, from 1 to 35 of 37 total language subsets for Global-MMLU, and from 3 to 31 of 43 for INCLUDE. Accuracy on the trained-language subsets rises by 0.098, 0.058, and 0.047, respectively, measured on the same models and checkpoints as the original items. The model-based reformulation is stronger still: 32 of 43 INCLUDE subsets pass the filter and accuracy rises by 0.075, and on Belebele every trained-language subset passes at every size and accuracy rises by 0.168. On INCLUDE,INCLUDE its gains widen with model size, from 0.015 at 175M to 0.075 at 1.7B, and the rewrite introduces no length cue, as the correct option is the single longest in 22.2\% of original items and 21.9\% of rewritten ones against 25\% by chance. Detailed results by benchmark are in Figure~\ref{fig:app_rq00_task_reformulation} (Appendix~\ref{app:rq00_task_reformulation}) and Figure~\ref{fig:app_chance_reformulation} (Appendix~\ref{app:chance-threshold}).
These above random gate results suggest that much of the weak scaling in these benchmarks comes from the answer format rather than the capability being tested.
Once models score the answer text directly, the same items provide substantially clearer signal across the scaling ladder, showing that benchmark predictability and usefulness for scaling predictions depend also on how it is evaluated.

The curves of the six model sizes do not coincide: at $10^{8}$--$10^{9}$ tokens, 40\% of the 90M cells are above chance against 60\% of the 1.7B cells, so exposure to the language is necessary but not sufficient, and the token count a family needs depends on the model size (Table~\ref{tab:rq0_thresholds}).
The original letter-format tasks fail the filter at nearly every token count reached in the sweep: the original INCLUDE stays at 5--11\% of its cells from $10^{7}$ tokens on, while its reformulated version reaches 99\% (Figure~\ref{fig:rq0}).

\textbf{Item-count dependence of the filter.} The margin a task needs above chance shrinks with its item count. The median required margins are 9.00 percentage points for nonparallel Global PIQA at 100 items per language, 2.44 for Belebele at 900, 0.74 for HellaSwag at about 9{,}000 and 0.60 for Global-MMLU at about 14{,}000. Over the six sizes from 90M to 1.7B, these families pass the filter in 37.5\%, 1.1\%, 100.0\% and 4.1\% of their cells, and the reformulated Belebele and Global-MMLU in 75.4\% and 73.9\% (Appendix~\ref{app:chance-threshold}). A family with few items is reported as unable to detect small departures from chance, not as unmeasurable.

\subsection{Agreement: How small and early can a proxy be while preserving the reference decision?}
\label{subsec:rq2}

We next ask how early in model development we can recover the decisions that would be obtained from a full evaluation.
\AR{Add setting of the analysis.}
We measure decision accuracy (Eq.~\ref{eq:decision-accuracy}) in the three ranking-agreement settings of Table~\ref{tab:ra_defs}: DA-size for RA-size, DA-ckpt for RA-checkpoint and DA-goal for RA-end-to-end.
The pairs are formed among the seed-1904 runs of every language setting, ladder and data build, from 90M to 1.7B, and the reference is the final checkpoint of the 1.7B run of each configuration.

%\CL{

% RESOLVED (authors, 2026-10-08): Figure~\ref{fig:rq2} keeps the 0.66 cut (rq2_da_all_above_66_either_transformation_mono_axis). The unfiltered twin is rq2_da_all_mono_axis.
We study decision reliability along two dimensions of model development: training progress and model scale, and analyze it from three complementary perspectives. First, we measure within-model training convergence by comparing the decision at each intermediate checkpoint to the decision made by the final checkpoint of the same model size, testing how early a model's eventual decision stabilizes during training (Figure~\ref{fig:rq2}b). Second, we measure full-scale decision prediction by comparing every intermediate checkpoint, across model sizes, to the fully trained largest model, testing how early smaller and partially trained models can recover the decision of the final reference model (Figure~\ref{fig:rq2}c). Third, we study scale convergence by comparing the final checkpoint of each model size to the final checkpoint of the largest model, quantifying how small a model can be while still reproducing decisions made at full scale (Figure~\ref{fig:rq2}a).

\textbf{Within-model training convergence.}
Figure~\ref{fig:rq2}b shows that decision reliability increases steadily over training when each checkpoint is compared to the final checkpoint of the same model.
Early checkpoints recover only around 61--68\% of the final decisions, while agreement rises substantially later in training, to 84--92\% at 4.5C, with smaller models generally stabilizing earlier than larger ones.
Part of this rise is persistence within a run rather than a decision. Two seeds of one configuration, which have nothing to decide, already agree on 78--80\% of the pairs at 90\% of training, against 82--86\% for real design pairs (Figure~\ref{fig:app_rq02_seed_uncertainty}, Appendix~\ref{app:rq02_decision_accuracy}).

\textbf{Predicting full-scale decisions from cheaper models.}
Figures~\ref{fig:rq2}a and~\ref{fig:rq2}c show that this within-model convergence does not translate into reliable prediction of the final 1.7B decisions.
Across fully trained smaller models, agreement with the 1.7B reference remains modest and non-monotonic with scale (Figure~\ref{fig:rq2}a), and additional training of smaller models yields only limited improvement in full-scale decision reliability (Figure~\ref{fig:rq2}c).
Without the reliability filter, on benchmark accuracy alone, DA-size is only 0.51--0.57 from 90M to 1B, while the per-language BPB of the 50 trained languages reaches 0.92--0.96 at every proxy except 600M, where it drops to 0.74 (Figure~\ref{fig:app_rq02_decision_accuracy}, Appendix~\ref{app:rq02_decision_accuracy}). At 600M the depth pairs are not size-matched: the shallow 600M model has 3.7\% more non-embedding parameters than the deep one and spends about 15\% more training compute (Table~\ref{tab:ladder}).
The 600M drop comes almost entirely from these pairs (Table~\ref{tab:app_rq03_flip_attribution}, Appendix~\ref{app:rq03_noise_and_snr}). On single-axis pairs, per-language BPB at 600M orders the depth decision against the 1.7B reference in 95\% of the (pair, language) comparisons, against at most 16\% for the language count, the data scheme and the temperature. Most of these depth comparisons differ at 600M by less than one seed standard deviation, so the 600M comparison cannot distinguish the two depths rather than measuring an opposite effect. The deep 600M run of seed 1904 is also the least favourable of its three seeds: with the other two seeds, the same depth pair flips on 15 and 43 of the 50 languages instead of all 50.
Together, these results suggest that the cross-scale gap cannot be closed by training cheaper proxy models longer alone.

In this experiment, we investigate first whether existing surrogate metrics remain predictive of DA in the multilingual setting and second, which surrogate provides a reliable signal at the smallest model scale.

We first test whether statistics proposed to characterize evaluation reliability can serve as surrogates for DA without requiring the reference model.
\textcolor{blue}{For each benchmark--languagebenchmark-language task and proxy size, we compute each surrogate using only information available at the proxy scale and measure its Spearman ($\rho$) association with DA}, i.e., agreement between the proxy-size ranking and the ranking at the 1.7B reference model.
Figure~\ref{fig:rq3} shows the Spearman ($\rho$) association with DA of different surrogate implementations across model sizes over all benchmarks that have passed the above-chance threshold, depicting how their predictive strength changes as the proxy scale increases and whether any surrogate remains consistently informative across scales.
We compare several families of surrogate statistics that can be computed without the reference model, including scaling-fit \(R^2\), SNR-based measures \citep{heineman_signal_2025} and their signal/noise components, margin above chance, and early-checkpoint ranking agreement.
Scaling-fit \(R^2\) measures how well benchmark performance follows a log-linear trend across the model sizes available up to the proxy point. Margin above chance measures how far the benchmark score is above random-guessing performance. Early-checkpoint ranking agreement measures how well the ranking of model variants at an early training checkpoint matches their ranking at the final checkpoint of the same model size.
For each benchmark--languagebenchmark-language task and proxy size, we compute the surrogate using only information available up to that proxy point and measure its Spearman association with DA.

\textbf{At what model scale does surrogate signal emerge?}
\textcolor{blue}{We next ask how early in model development a surrogate becomes informative about DA.}
Using the same analysis in Figure~\ref{fig:rq3}, we compare surrogate--DAsurrogate-DA associations across increasing proxy model sizes, from 90M to 1B.
This allows us to test whether useful surrogate signal is already present at the smallest scale and whether it strengthens or remains stable as model scale increases.

sections/05_conclusions.tex

@@ -1,7 +1,7 @@
\section{Conclusions}
\label{sec:conclusions}

We study when multilingual evaluation supports decisions during pretraining. Predictable learning curves, evidence above chance, and agreement with a larger model answer different questions. HellaSwag, LAMBADA, and per-language BPB track scale consistently, while reformulating letter-based tasks as answer continuations reveals signal that their original protocols suppress. Across the 12-checkpoint10-checkpoint evaluation grid, BPB rankings transfer more consistently to the 1.7B reference than the aggregate benchmark rankings, but early agreement can reverse during training. Cross-task transfer is limited, and the most informative SNR definition varies by language and by whether the decision concerns model size or training progress. These findings favor task- and language-specific checks over a single benchmark score or universal proxy threshold.

%Our next steps address two questions left open by this analysis. First, we will test how predictivity depends on the design intervention, comparing language selection, sampling temperature, second-language choice, and architecture while estimating uncertainty in the reference ranking from repeated runs. Second, we will test whether scaling behavior and decision reliability transfer to held-out languages, with explicit separation between languages absent from pretraining and languages withheld from fitting the predictor. These studies will also provide held-out decisions for calibrating surrogate-based selection and stopping rules, extending the present descriptive evidence toward actionable guidance for multilingual model development.

- one tokenizer for every model and language

- English is half of every multilingual mixture, so only the share of the other languages varies

- single seed for most cells, and one 1.7B reference run per configuration

- many benchmarks are machine translated

- checkpoint noise is read in one window, the last 20\% of training during the cooldown

- the model-based reformulation relies on a proprietary model

- acknowledge high compute cost, but we hope this will help the community to take informed decisions, launch useful ablations and avoid wasting compute on noisy evals

- energy use and emissions of the sweep are not estimated

- low-resource languages have fewer benchmarks and less web data, which may not represent their speakers

- release of models trained on web data without safety tuning, for research use only

Point out any strong assumptions and how robust your results are to violations of these assumptions (e.g., independence assumptions, noiseless settings, model well-specification, asymptotic approximations only held locally). Reflect on how these assumptions might be violated in practice and what the implications would be.
Reflect on the scope of your claims, e.g., if you only tested your approach on a few datasets, languages, or did a few runs. In general, empirical results often depend on implicit assumptions, which should be articulated. Reflect on the factors that influence the performance of your approach. For example, a speech-to-text system might not be able to be reliably used to provide closed captions for online lectures because it fails to handle technical jargon.

In general, we advise authors to use their best judgement and recognize that individual actions in favor of transparency play an important role in developing norms that preserve the integrity of the community. Reviewers will be specifically instructed to not penalize honesty concerning limitations.

Examples of risks include potential malicious or unintended harmful effects and uses (e.g., disinformation, generating fake profiles, surveillance), environmental impact (e.g., training huge models), fairness considerations (e.g., deployment of technologies that could further disadvantage or exclude historically disadvantaged groups), privacy considerations (e.g., a paper on model/data stealing), and security considerations (e.g., adversarial attacks). We expect many papers to be foundational research and not tied to particular applications, let alone deployments. For example, the authors can emphasize how their systems are intended to be used, how they can safeguard their systems against misuse, or propose future research directions.

sections/app_02_data_mixtures.tex

@@ -4,12 +4,12 @@
\textbf{Language lists.} We generate the language lists from the FineWeb-2 per-language byte counts and from the benchmarks available in the evaluation harness. \emph{Scheme A} ranks the FineWeb-2 subsets by training-split UTF-8 bytes, excluding the unidentified-language subsets, and takes the top $K-1$. The lists are nested. The $K=2$ list (Russian) is a subset of the $K=8$ list (Russian, Chinese, German, Japanese, Spanish, French, Italian), which is a subset of the $K=15$, $K=30$ and $K=50$ lists. We also generated a 100-language list with the same rule, but we did not train it, as explained below. In that list, the eight subsets among the top 99 that have no benchmark in the harness were replaced by the next subsets by bytes with at least two benchmark families. \emph{Scheme B} builds diversity-first lists at $K \in \{8, 15, 30\}$ within the top 49. It first takes the largest subset of each script not yet covered, then of each language family not yet covered, then the remaining subsets by bytes. At $K=8$, the scheme-B list spans seven scripts against four for scheme A. At $K=30$, it spans 14 scripts and 12 families against 10 and 10. At $K=2$ there is no list to diversify. There, the scheme-B and scheme-C builds (\emph{ZH} and \emph{ES}) replace Russian with Chinese or Spanish. At $K=1$ there is no second language. There, the scheme-B and scheme-C builds replace the English corpus instead. Scheme B (\emph{DCLMP}) uses the same DCLM pipeline without its educational-quality filter. Scheme C (\emph{FWEB}) uses FineWeb restricted to the crawls up to 2022, which is DCLM's own Common Crawl window.
Table~\ref{tab:app-languages} lists the 99 FineWeb-2 languages of the sweep. For each language, it gives the language setting at which the language enters the scheme-A and scheme-B lists, and the number of benchmark families that the harness offers for it.

\input{sections/app_02_table_languages}\input{sections/rqs/app_02_table_languages}


\textbf{Mixture composition.} Every multilingual setting blends English and FineWeb-2 50/50 at training time, with the blend weights of the Megatron data loader. The blend uses one English dataset and one FineWeb-2 dataset per (scheme, setting). Within the FineWeb-2 half, language $i$ receives a share $q_i \propto p_i^{1/T}$, where $p_i$ is its estimated token proportion in the corpus. Scheme A allocates at $T=1$, in proportion to the corpus. The FineWeb-2 byte distribution is extreme. On the planned 100-language list at $T=1$, the largest language would take 28\% of the multilingual half and the smallest 0.0017\%. At the 90M rung, 66 of the 99 languages would receive under 10M tokens. The flattened scheme AT3 allocates at $T=3$, which would lift the median language of that build from 90M to 373M tokens. We dropped the $K=100$ setting from the grid. Instead, we train the temperature intervention on the scheme-A lists at $K=50$ (both architectures). Where flattening starves no language, we also train it at $K=30$ and $K=15$ (deep only). At these two settings, the smallest language keeps 343M and 1.2B tokens at $T=1$ under the 1.7B budget.

\textbf{Build sizing and repetition.} Each FineWeb-2 dataset is built to half of the largest budget that trains on it, plus 10\% headroom. This gives 92B tokens for the 1.7B rung and 165B at the settings where the 3B rung trains. The ZH build (52B) was sized when that scheme stopped at 1B, and its 1.7B cell reads it unchanged. The English dataset is built once, to 184B tokens. The builder never repeats a document, so a language that runs out of data gives a smaller build. Smaller models draw a shuffled fraction of the same build, so within a setting all sizes see the same language proportions. We record two departures from a single pass and carry them into the analysis. First, no single FineWeb-2 subset can feed the 1.7B rung at $K=2$. The Russian build has 72.8B tokens, against the 83.6B that a 1.7B model draws from the multilingual half, so the 1.7B model at $K=2$ sees Russian about 1.15 times. The Chinese build (52.0B tokens) and the Spanish build (23.9B, all the Spanish that the source holds) are smaller still. At 1.7B, Chinese is seen 1.61 times and Spanish 3.51 times (0.65, 0.91 and 1.98 times for Russian, Chinese and Spanish at 1B). Repetition stays below 1.7 epochs across the Russian and Chinese ladders. For Spanish, it rises from 0.7 at 350M to 3.5 at 1.7B, so any comparison of the $K=2$ schemes states these epoch counts. Second, the $K=15$ and $K=50$ builds of scheme A and the $K=15$ build of scheme B were first built to 52B. They were extended to 92B when the 1.7B rung was added at those settings. The extension continues the token stream of each language from the same starting point. The six 1.7B cells there therefore read a superset of what their proxies read, extended with later CommonCrawl dumps. %\footnote{Verifieddumps.\footnote{Verified byte for byte: every language section of each 52B build is a prefix of the corresponding 92B build.}

\textbf{Validation set.} One fixed validation set is shared by every model and every scheme. It covers the 99 FineWeb-2 languages of the $K=100$ list plus English. For each language, it takes the first 5M tokens of the first parquet file of that language. It takes at most 30\% of the rows of the file, so that single-file languages keep training data. The build records the token count of each language, its UTF-8 byte count and the number of leading rows assigned to validation. Every training build reads this manifest and skips exactly those rows, so training and validation are disjoint by construction. Since there is one validation build for the whole sweep, bits-per-byte is comparable across sizes, language settings and schemes.

\textbf{Task selection.} We select the registered tasks whose language is in the training mixture. Task counts can exceed family counts. For the resource-ranked lists, the number of tasks grows with $K$: \ntasksKone at $K=1$, \ntasksKtwo at $K=2$, \ntasksKeight at $K=8$, \ntasksKfifteen at $K=15$, \ntasksKthirty at $K=30$, and \ntasksKfifty at $K=50$. These counts cover the whole evaluated group of Table~\ref{tab:benchmark-families}, that is, the multilingual families, the reformulated variants of the letter-format families (\S\ref{subsec:rq1}) and the English-text benchmarks. Every trained language is covered by at least one family. For the seed-1904 deep scheme-A ladder,cells of scheme A at every $K$ and of scheme B at $K=8$, we also run every task in every language, independent of the training mixture. This lets us read cross-lingual transfer on the benchmarks as well as on bits-per-byte. 

\textbf{Language coverage.} Every trained language is covered by at least one benchmark family. English is covered by 14 of the multilingual families plus 11 of the English-text benchmarks. Of the 100 languages of Table~\ref{tab:app-languages}, eighteen have exactly one family and fourteen have two. The languages with a single family are covered by Belebele (12), MultiBLiMP (4) or Global PIQA (2) alone. For those languages, the per-language benchmark read rests on one task. Counting tasks instead of families, a cell is evaluated on \ntasksKone tasks at $K=1$, \ntasksKtwo at $K=2$, \ntasksKeight at $K=8$, \ntasksKfifteen at $K=15$, \ntasksKthirty at $K=30$ and \ntasksKfifty at $K=50$, including the reformulated variants and the English-only benchmarks. Two IrokoBench families (AfriXNLI and AfriMMLU) are wired but disabled because of a loading defect in the pinned harness. Enabling them again would add a second or third human-translated family for Amharic, Igbo, Kinyarwanda, Southern Sotho, Xhosa and Zulu.


\textbf{Harness settings.} All benchmarks run through a fork of the LM Evaluation Harness \citep{gao_framework_2021}. We install it from a pinned shared checkout, and each evaluation job records the source commit. We use the vLLM backend \citep{kwon_efficient_2023}, a maximum context of 4,096 tokens, a beginning-of-sequence token before every prompt, no chat template, and the default prompt and number of in-context examples of each task. We report accuracy for multiple-choice and completion tasks and exact match for the generative ones. The exception is the tasks whose options are answer strings scored as continuations, that is, the cloze-format originals (ARC, HellaSwag, OpenBookQA, MathQA and Global PIQA) and the reformulated variants, which we score with length-normalised accuracy. We aggregate sub-tasks (for example the MMLU subjects) to their family with the harness's own grouping. Base models at these sizes are at chance on several families. The above-chance threshold of Appendix~\ref{app:chance-threshold} decides, per task and size, whether such a cell enters the analysis.

sections/app_04_chance_threshold.tex

@@ -2,7 +2,6 @@
\section{Above chance threshold}
\label{app:chance-threshold}

\subsection{Threshold definition}

For each language task $b_\ell$ and model size $N$, we test whether the observed accuracy is distinguishable from random guessing.
For a task with $m_b$ answer options, chance accuracy is $1/m_b$. We read the option count from the evaluated samples where available, and from the benchmark's specification otherwise. Where the option count varies across items, chance is the mean of $1/m$ over the items (TruthfulQA mc1, 0.2253). TruthfulQA mc2 scores probability mass instead of a single pick, so its chance level is the mean share of true options (0.449).

@@ -18,11 +17,16 @@ A $(b_\ell, N)$ cell is retained if at least 50\% of these models are above chan
When models trained on the benchmark language are available, only those models enter the decision. Otherwise, all evaluated models of that size enter it.
The resulting mask is the gate that every analysis reads. A gated cell keeps its place in every grid and table but carries no value. A scaling fit uses only the sizes where the task passes. A ranking against the reference also requires the task to pass at the reference size.
Measurements without a meaningful chance baseline, such as bits per byte and training loss, are not filtered by this procedure.
This filtering step only decides whether a benchmark gives measurable signal at a given scale. It does not assess whether the benchmark can reliably rank model variants.

\textbf{Per-benchmark threshold.} The Wilson bound accounts for the number of evaluation examples, so the amount by which accuracy must exceed chance varies across tasks.
Smaller evaluation sets require a larger margin above chance, while larger sets can support smaller but more reliable departures from chance.
For example, the median required margins are 9.00 percentage points for nonparallel Global PIQA (100 items per language), 2.44 for Belebele (900), 0.74 for HellaSwag (about 9,000), and 0.60 for Global-MMLU (about 14,000).

\textbf{The formulation effect.}
The corresponding fractions of benchmark-language-size cells that pass the filter over the six sizes from 90M to 1.7B are 37.5\%, 1.1\%, 100.0\% and 4.1\%, respectively. The reformulated variants of Belebele and Global-MMLU pass in 75.4\% and 73.9\% of their cells.

\section{Signal\section{Signal, Noise and Noise}
\label{app:methodology-snr}Surrogates of Decision Accuracy}
\label{subsec:methodology-snr}
\label{app:surrogates}

The signal-to-noise ratio (SNR) summarizes a benchmark'sthe reliability of a benchmark as the ratio between signal,signal and noise. Signal is how well a benchmark separates models of similar compute, and noise,compute. Noise is the variability in scores across training checkpoints or stochastic runs.
We define it as
\begin{equation}
\text{SNR} = \frac{\text{Signal}}{\text{Noise}}.
\end{equation}
A higher SNR means that score differences across models are large relative to thebenchmark's intrinsic variability.variability of the benchmark. The framework is not tied to a particular definition of signal or noise definition,noise, so we evaluate several candidatesevery signal and select the pair withnoise of the strongest empirical correlation to DA.released code (\S\ref{app:sur-signals} and \S\ref{app:sur-noise}) and score each ratio against decision accuracy (\S\ref{app:sur-results}).

A \emph{surrogate} is a statistic of a benchmark that is computed on the proxy size alone. It is meant to tell, without training the reference, whether the ranking of the proxy can be trusted.
This appendix defines every surrogate we compute. These are the signal-to-noise ratio of \citet{heineman_signal_2025} with all its signal and noise variants (\S\ref{app:sur-snr}--\ref{app:sur-noise}), the task-selection criteria of FineTasks (\S\ref{app:sur-finetasks}), and a catalogue of statistics from the literature on evaluation reliability (\S\ref{app:sur-catalogue}).
For each we give the formula as implemented, the paper it comes from, and, where the two differ, the difference.
\S\ref{app:sur-scoring} describes how surrogates are scored and \S\ref{app:sur-results} gives the summary table of correlations.

\subsection{Signal candidates.}
\label{app:measuring-signal}\subsection{Notation and the truths}
\label{app:sur-notation}

GivenA task is scored on the final checkpoints$V$ design variants of one proxy size.
Variant $a$ has a training runscurve $x_a(t)$ at similar compute $C_\text{final}$, we considerthe ten evaluated tenths $t \in \{0.1,\dots,1\}$ of its run, a final score $x_a = x_a(1)$, and a \emph{noise window} $W_a = \{x_a(t) : t \in \{0.80, 0.85, 0.90, 0.95, 1\}\}$ with mean $\bar{w}_a$ and checkpoint noise $s_a$, the residual standard deviation of $W_a$ around a least-squares line through its five candidate signal definitions to capturepoints (three degrees of freedom). Scores still rise over the learning-rate decay, and the raw standard deviation of $W_a$ would count that rise as noise. We keep the raw standard deviation $s^{0}_a$ (divide by $n_c$) as a different notionvariant.
$\mathcal{P}$ is the set of variant pairs (every pair, or the pairs that differ in one design axis), $n$ the number of how spread out model scores are.test items, $c$ the chance level, $\bar{x}$ the mean final score over variants, and $\Phi$ the standard normal distribution function.
Scores are oriented so that higher is better (bits per byte enter with a flipped sign).

Variance is the average squared distance from the mean. Mean distance is the average pairwise distance between points. Relative standard deviation is the standard deviation divided by the mean. Star discrepancy is the largest difference between any point\paragraph{Decision accuracy.} For two score vectors $u$ and $y$ over the uniform distribution. Dispersion is the largest pairwise distance,same variants,
\begin{equation}
\text{Dispersion}(C_\text{final})\mathrm{DA}(u, y) = \max_{i \neq j} \|c_i\frac{1}{|\mathcal{P}|} \sum_{(a,b) \in \mathcal{P}} \mathbf{1}\big[\mathrm{sign}(u_a - c_j\|.u_b) = \mathrm{sign}(y_a - y_b)\big],
\label{eq:sur-da}
\end{equation}
the definition of \citet{magnusson_datadecide_2025} and \citet{heineman_signal_2025}.
A surrogate is scored against three truths. DA-size is $\mathrm{DA}(x^{\text{proxy}}, x^{\text{ref}})$ between the final rankings of the proxy and of the reference. DA-goal is $\mathrm{DA}(x^{\text{proxy}}(t), x^{\text{ref}})$ for $t < 1$. DA-ckpt is $\mathrm{DA}(x^{\text{proxy}}(t), x^{\text{proxy}})$, the proxy against its own final checkpoint.
Each is also measured as Kendall's $\tau_b$ and Spearman's $\rho$ on the same two vectors.

\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=15pt,noitemsep,topsep=0pt]
    \item \textbf{Variance} measures average squared distance\emph{Differences from the mean: $\text{Var}(C_\text{final}) = \frac{1}{n} \sum_{i=1}^{n} \|c_i - \bar{c}\|^2$
    \item \textbf{Mean distance} measures average pairwise distance between points: $\text{Mean Dist}(C_\text{final}) = \frac{2}{n(n-1)} \sum_{i < j} \|c_i - c_j\|$
    \item \textbf{Relative standard deviation}, orsource.}
\citet{magnusson_datadecide_2025} take the coefficientmean of variation, measures the standard deviation divided bythree seeds as the mean: $\text{Rel. Std.}(C_\text{final}) = \frac{\sqrt{\text{Var}(C_\text{final})}}{\text{Mean}(C_\text{final})}$
    \item \textbf{Star Discrepancy} measures the largest difference between any pointtarget and average the uniform distribution: $\text{Discrepancy}(C_\text{final})decision accuracy of three single-seed proxies. Our grid has one seed per variant on both sides.
Neither paper treats ties, and \citet{heineman_signal_2025} derive $\tau = \sup_{t \in [0,1]} \left| \frac{1}{n} \sum_{i=1}^n \mathbf{1}\{c_i \leq t\}2\,\mathrm{DA} - t \right|$. 
    \item \textbf{Dispersion} measures the largest difference between any two points, or the largest unfilled space in the range of performance: $\text{Dispersion}(C_\text{final})1$ under that assumption.
Under Eq.~\ref{eq:sur-da}, a pair tied on both sides agrees and a pair tied on one side is a miss. So with $C$ concordant, $D$ discordant, $T_2$ doubly tied and $T_1$ singly tied pairs, $2\,\mathrm{DA} - 1 = \max_{i \neq j} \|c_i\tau_a + (T_2 - c_j\|$. 
\end{itemize}T_1)/|\mathcal{P}|$.

\subsection{Noise candidates.}
\label{app:measuring-noise}

We consider four sources of noise that the framework can in principle account for.\subsection{The signal-to-noise ratio}
\label{app:sur-snr}

%\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=15pt,noitemsep,topsep=0pt]\citet{heineman_signal_2025} define, for the final scores $m_j$ of the small models and the last $n_c$ checkpoints $m_{j,1},\dots,m_{j,n_c}$ of each,
\begin{equation}
\text{Signal} = \frac{\max_{j,k} |m_j - m_k|}{\bar{m}}, \qquad
\text{Noise}(m_j) = \frac{1}{\bar{m}_j}\sqrt{\frac{1}{n_c - 1}\sum_{i=1}^{n_c}(m_{j,i} - \bar{m}_j)^2}, \qquad
\mathrm{SNR} = \frac{\text{Signal}}{\text{Noise}},
\label{eq:sur-snr-paper}
\end{equation}
with the noise averaged over the small models and $n_c = 5$.
They report $R = 0.791$ ($R^2 = 0.626$) between this SNR and decision accuracy over their benchmarks.

\textbf{Seed noise} is the standard deviation ofWe compute the final checkpoint across training runsSNR withdifferent random seeds.
%\textbf{Seed noise.} To measure the noise{} introducedcode released with that paper, which differs from changing the random seed initialization between training runs, we can computeEq.~\ref{eq:sur-snr-paper} in three ways.
With the raw standard deviation $s^{0}_a$ of $W_a$, which the final checkpoint from multiple training runs with different random seeds. To estimate seed noise{}, we train $M$ models using the same configuration, and average the scores over the final $n$ checkpoints of $T$ total training checkpoints to smooth the checkpoint-to-checkpoint noise{}, then compute the standard deviation:released code uses:
\begin{equation}
\text{Seed noise}(M)\text{Signal}_{\text{code}} = \sigma(M),\quad M_i\frac{\max_{a,b} |x_a - x_b|}{\frac{1}{V}\sum_a \bar{w}_a}, \qquad
\text{Noise}_{\text{code}} = \textstyle\frac{1}{n} \sum_{j=T-n+1}^{T} U(t_j)\frac{\frac{1}{V}\sum_a s^{0}_a}{\frac{1}{V}\sum_a \bar{w}_a}.
\label{eq:sur-snr-code}
\end{equation}
(i) The signal is divided by the mean of the window means, not by the mean of the final scores.
(ii) The noise is a ratio of means over models, not a mean of per-model ratios.
(iii) The standard deviation divides by $n_c$, not $n_c - 1$, which makes the noise smaller by $\sqrt{4/5}$ at $n_c = 5$ and the SNR larger by 1.118.
Our window is five checkpoints spread over the last 20\,\% of the run, where the original uses the last five saved checkpoints. Our noise replaces $s^{0}_a$ in Eq.~\ref{eq:sur-snr-code} by the detrended $s_a$, and the raw $s^{0}_a$ remains as a variant.

\subsection{Signal variants}
\label{app:sur-signals}
\label{app:measuring-signal}

Table~\ref{tab:sur-signals} lists the 22 signal definitions of the released code.
Each is divided by a noise of \S\ref{app:sur-noise} to give an SNR.
Table~3 of \citet{heineman_signal_2025} prints a formula for each. The last column records where the code computes something else.
We write $c_i$ for the final scores, $\bar{c}$ for their mean, $\bar{w}$ for the mean of the window means, $c_{(i)}$ for the $i$-th smallest score with $c_{(0)} = 0$, and $\tilde{c}_i = (c_i - \min c)/(\max c - \min c)$.

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\scriptsize
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.19\linewidth}p{0.36\linewidth}p{0.39\linewidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Signal (code name)} & \textbf{As implemented} & \textbf{Against the printed formula} \\
\midrule
\texttt{rel\_std} & $\sigma(c)/\bar{c}$, population $\sigma$ & matches \\
\texttt{rel\_dispersion} & $(\max c - \min c)/\bar{w}$ & printed with $\bar{c}$. The paper's headline signal \\
\texttt{dispersion} & $\max_{i,j}|c_i - c_j|$ & matches, identical to \texttt{range} \\
\texttt{dispersion\_shifted} & $\max_{i,j}|\tilde{c}_i - \tilde{c}_j| = 1$ & constant, so its SNR is $1/\text{noise}$ \\
\texttt{range} & $\max c - \min c$ & matches \\
\texttt{mpd} & $\frac{2}{V(V-1)}\sum_{i<j}|c_i - c_j|$ & printed with $1/V^2$ over all $(i,j)$ \\
\texttt{rel\_mpd} & \texttt{mpd}$/\bar{w}$ & printed with $\bar{c}$ \\
\texttt{mpsd} & $\frac{2}{V(V-1)}\sum_{i<j}(c_i - c_j)^2$ & printed with $1/V^2$. It equals twice the sample variance \\
\texttt{rel\_mpsd} & \texttt{mpsd}$/\bar{w}$ & printed as divided by $\bar{c}^2$ \\
\texttt{mad} & $\mathrm{median}_i\,|c_i - \mathrm{median}(c)|$ & matches \\
\texttt{aad} & $\frac{1}{V}\sum_i |c_i - \bar{c}|$ & matches \\
\texttt{dist\_std} & $\sigma\big(\{|c_i - c_j|\}_{i<j}\big)$ & printed as $\frac{1}{V}\sum_i (c_i - \bar{c})$, which is zero \\
\texttt{rms\_deviation} & $\sqrt{\frac{1}{V}\sum_i (c_i - \bar{c})^2}$ & matches. It is the root of the paper's ``variance'' \\
\texttt{quartile\_deviation} & $(Q_3 - Q_1)/2$ & matches \\
\texttt{iqr} & $(Q_3 - Q_1)/\bar{w}$ & printed without the division \\
\texttt{gini} & Gini coefficient of $\tilde{c}$ & printed on the raw scores \\
\texttt{discrepancy} & centred $L_2$ discrepancy of $c$ in $[0,1]$ & printed as $\max_t |F_V(t) - t|$, a sup-norm \\
\texttt{star\_discrepancy} & $\frac{1}{V+1}\sum_{i=0}^{V}\big|\tfrac{i}{V} - c_{(i)}\big|$ & printed as $\sup_t |F_V(t) - t|$. The code takes a mean \\
\texttt{star\_discrepancy\_shifted} & the same on $\tilde{c}$ & as above \\
\texttt{rel\_star\_discrepancy} & \texttt{star\_discrepancy}$/\bar{w}$ & printed as divided by $F(t)$ \\
\texttt{tukey} & $\mathrm{IQR}\big(\min(F_V(c_i),\, 1 - F_V(c_i^-))\big)/\bar{w}$ & the halfspace depth, with its own noise (\S\ref{app:sur-noise}) \\
\texttt{projection} & $\mathrm{IQR}\big((1 + |c_i - \mathrm{med}(c)|/\mathrm{MAD}(c))^{-1}\big)/\bar{w}$ & the projection depth, with its own noise \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{The 22 signal definitions (\texttt{snr/snr\_variants.py}, from the code released with \citet{heineman_signal_2025}). The four discrepancy signals read scores as points of $[0,1]$ and are computed on benchmarks only.}
\label{tab:sur-signals}
\end{table}

\textbf{Data order} noise is the equivalent across runs that vary the orderAppendix A.4 of training documents. 
%\textbf{Data Order noise{}.} This is noise{} introduced from changing\citet{heineman_signal_2025} also names five measures in prose (variance, mean distance, relative standard deviation, star discrepancy, dispersion). They correspond to \texttt{rms\_deviation} squared, \texttt{mpd}, \texttt{rel\_std}, \texttt{star\_discrepancy} and \texttt{dispersion}.
In their Table~3 the order of sampled documents from the training data. We estimate$R^2$ with decision accuracy is 0.57 for relative dispersion and relative standard deviation, 0.55 for relative mean pairwise distance, 0.48 for the data order noise{} usinginterquartile range, and below 0.10 for the same method as seed noise{}.Gini, discrepancy and depth signals.

\textbf{Total variation} is the average change in metric score across training checkpoints minus an improvement term.
%\textbf{Total Variation.} To measure the checkpoint-to-checkpoint noise{} throughout an entire training run, we measure the total variation of the intermediate training checkpoints on the downstream benchmark. We measure total variation as the average change in metric score across $T$ training checkpoints minus an improvement term:\subsection{Noise variants}
\label{app:sur-noise}
\label{app:measuring-noise}
\label{app:total-variation}
\begin{equation}
\text{Total Variation} = \textstyle\frac{1}{T} \sum_{t=1}^{T} |U(t) - U(t-1)| - \frac{1}{T}(U(T) - U(0))
\end{equation}

\textbf{Checkpoint-to-checkpoint noise} is proposed bySix noises enter the signal $\times$ noise grid ($22 \times 6 = 132$ ratios, each signal and each noise also scored alone).

\begin{itemize}[itemsep=1pt,topsep=2pt]
\item \textbf{Checkpoint noise, relative} (\texttt{ckpt\_rel}): $\text{Noise}_{\text{code}}$ of Eq.~\ref{eq:sur-snr-code} with the detrended $s_a$ in place of $s^{0}_a$, the checkpoint-to-checkpoint noise of \citet{heineman_signal_2025} as a proxy when the first three are too expensivereleased up to estimate at large scalethe detrending.
\item \textbf{Checkpoint noise, absolute} (\texttt{ckpt\_abs}): $\frac{1}{V}\sum_a s_a$, their Eq.~7, $\sigma(\{U(t_j)\}_{j=T-n_c+1}^{T})$, detrended and depends only onaveraged over variants.
\item \textbf{Depth noises} (\texttt{tukey\_depth}, \texttt{projection\_depth}): $\frac{1}{V}\sum_a s_a$ divided by the mean depth of the window means $\bar{w}_a$ among the final $n$ training checkpoints:scores. These are the noises the released code pairs with the two depth signals. The paper does not print them.
\item \textbf{Benchmark noise, relative} (\texttt{kfold\_rel}): the mean over models of the relative standard deviation of a model's score across $k = 5$ disjoint folds of the test items,
\begin{equation}
\text{Checkpoint noise}\text{Noise}_{k\text{-fold}} = \sigma\big(\{U(t_j)\}_{j=T-k+1}^{T}\big).\frac{1}{V}\sum_a \frac{1}{x_a}\sqrt{\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=1}^{k}(m_{a,i} - x_a)^2},
\label{eq:sur-kfold}
\end{equation}
On our ladder both checkpoint noise, overwith $m_{a,i}$ the last five evaluated checkpoints of each run,fold scores. This is our own definition. It needs one evaluation run and seed noise,no intermediate checkpoint.
The ladder report carries no per-item outputs, so we use its expectation over the cells trained with three seeds, are measurable, and \S\ref{subsec:rq4} reports every intervention's effect against the seed noise.

\textbf{Benchmark noise}random partitions. For $n$ binary items at accuracy $p$, a fold is the average relative standard deviation of scores across $k = 5$ disjoint foldsa sample of the evaluation set:$n/k$ items without replacement, hence
\begin{equation}
\text{Noise}(M)\mathbb{E}\Big[\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=1}^{k}(m_i - p)^2\Big] = \frac{1}{|M|} \sum_{m \in M} \frac{\sqrt{\tfrac{1}{k}\sum_{i=1}^{k}(m_i\frac{p(1-p)}{n/k}\cdot\frac{n - n/k}{n - \bar{m})^2}}{\bar{m}}1} = \frac{p(1-p)(k-1)}{n-1},
\label{eq:sur-kfold-closed}
\end{equation}
where $m_1, \dots, m_k$and the noise of a model is the root of this divided by $p$. The value of $k$ rescales every task alike and moves no ranking.
\item \textbf{Benchmark noise, absolute} (\texttt{kfold\_abs}): Eq.~\ref{eq:sur-kfold} without the division by $x_a$.
\end{itemize}

We consider the $k$-fold benchmark noise beside the checkpoint noise for two reasons. First, it needs one evaluation run of a model and no intermediate checkpoint, so it applies to any public model. Second, on its own it correlates slightly more strongly with decision accuracy than the checkpoint noise ($\rho = -0.46$ against $\rho = -0.44$ with DA-size). As the denominator of the SNR, however, the detrended checkpoint noise does better for 16 of the 22 signals (\S\ref{app:sur-results}).

\citet{heineman_signal_2025} define three further noises that validate the checkpoint noise and do not enter the grid.
\emph{Seed noise} is $\sigma(M)$ over runs that differ in the seed, with $M_i = \frac{1}{n_c}\sum_{j=T-n_c+1}^{T} U(t_j)$. \emph{Data-order noise} is the same over runs that differ in the order of documents.
\emph{Total variation} is $\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}|U(t) - U(t-1)| - \frac{1}{T}(U(T) - U(0))$.
They report $R^2$ of 0.82, 0.86 and 0.95 between these and the checkpoint noise.
On our ladder the seed noise is the sample standard deviation of the final score over the three seeds of the replicated cells. We use it to size effects (Appendix~\ref{app:rq03_noise_and_snr}), not as a surrogate.
In the terms of \citet{wang_measuring_2025}, checkpoint and seed noise are prediction noise, the $k$-fold noise is data noise, and a training curve never shows the latter because it re-evaluates the same items.

\subsection{The FineTasks criteria}
\label{app:sur-finetasks}

FineTasks \citep{kydlicek_finetasks_nodate} selects tasks with four criteria computed along training, defined with formulas in the FineWeb2 paper \citep{penedo_fineweb2_2025}.
With $M$ the runs, $m(s)$ the score of run $m$ at step $s$, $\sigma_s$ the standard deviation over runs at step $s$ and $b$ the random baseline:

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\scriptsize
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.14\linewidth}p{0.33\linewidth}p{0.47\linewidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Criterion} & \textbf{As implemented} & \textbf{Against the source} \\
\midrule
Monotonicity & $\frac{1}{|M|}\sum_{m}\rho_{\text{Spearman}}(s, m(s)) \geq 0.5$ & matches \\
Low noise (``SNR'') & $\dfrac{\frac{1}{|M|}\sum_m m(1)}{\frac{1}{S}\sum_s \sigma_s} > 20$ & the blog's definition. The FineWeb2 paper uses $\frac{1}{S}\sum_s \mu_s/\sigma_s$, with $\sigma_s$ the population deviation over four seed runs. Ours is the sample deviation over the design variants \\
Non-randomness & $\dfrac{\max_m m(1) - b}{\sigma_{1}} > 3$ & the source averages $\sigma_s$ over the last five steps and takes it from the seed runs \\
Ordering consistency & mean $\tau_a$ of the run rankings at consecutive steps in the second half of training & matches. The source sets no threshold \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{The FineTasks criteria (\texttt{finetasks\_criteria.py}). Their ``noise'' is the spread across runs at one step, which the SNR framework calls signal: their SNR is the inverse of our relative signal, averaged over the run.}
\label{tab:sur-finetasks}
\end{table}

The first analysis of statistics beyond SNR (\texttt{analyze.py}, \texttt{finetasks\_criteria.py}) also scored the following proxy-only statistics, which have no external source. They are the $R^2$ of the linear fit of the score on $\log N$ over the rungs up to the proxy, the margin $\bar{x} - c$ above chance, and the proxy's own DA-ckpt at 10\,\%, 50\,\% and 90\,\%. They are also the gain of the mean score between 50\,\% (or 90\,\%) and the final checkpoint, the slope of the mean score over the last three tenths, and the earliest tenth from which $\tau_a$ with the final ranking stays at or above 0.8. Finally, they are the mean $\tau_a$ between the rankings inside the noise window, the variance of the rank of a variant over the second half of training, the standard deviation of the final scores across variants, and the item count.

\subsection{The catalogue of statistics from the literature}
\label{app:sur-catalogue}

Tables~\ref{tab:sur-separation}--\ref{tab:sur-agreement} define the catalogue (\texttt{catalogue.py}).
Most of these statistics were proposed for a different unit of replication, such as test items, topics, seeds or public models.
We port each one to what one proxy size offers, that is, the checkpoints of its noise window and the rungs below it. The last column states what we changed.
``Own'' marks a statistic that the cited paper motivates but does not define.
Throughout, $s_a$ is the checkpoint noise of $W_a$ (\S\ref{app:sur-notation}), $s = \sqrt{\frac{1}{V}\sum_a s_a^2}$ the pooled one, $d_{ab} = |x_a - x_b|$, $z_{ab} = d_{ab}/\sqrt{s_a^2 + s_b^2}$ (capped at 50), and $\mathrm{se}_p = \sqrt{p(1-p)/n}$ the binomial standard error \citep{miller_adding_2024}.
$\mathrm{MS_B}$ and $\mathrm{MS_W}$ are the between-variant and within-variant mean squares of the $V \times 5$ matrix of window scores.

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\scriptsize
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.215\linewidth}p{0.28\linewidth}p{0.14\linewidth}p{0.285\linewidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Statistic} & \textbf{Definition} & \textbf{Source} & \textbf{Relation to the source} \\
\midrule
\texttt{gap\_over\_noise} & $\mathrm{median}_{(a,b)}\, z_{ab}$ & \citet{heineman_signal_2025} & own: the SNR read pair by pair \\
\texttt{resolved\_pairs\_noise} & share of pairs with $z_{ab} > 1.96$ & \citet{sakai_evaluating_2006} & discriminative power, with checkpoint noise and an unpaired normal cut-off in place of the paired bootstrap over topics \\
\texttt{retest\_agreement} & $\mathrm{mean}_{(a,b)}\,\Phi(z_{ab})^2 + \Phi(-z_{ab})^2$ & \citet{voorhees_effect_2002} & one minus the swap rate under a normal model. The source counts swaps between disjoint topic sets, binned by score difference \\
\texttt{icc\_window} & $\dfrac{\mathrm{MS_B} - \mathrm{MS_W}}{\mathrm{MS_B} + 4\,\mathrm{MS_W}}$ & \citet{shrout_intraclass_1979} & ICC(1,1), exact \\
\texttt{g\_coefficient} & $\dfrac{\mathrm{MS_B} - \mathrm{MS_W}}{\mathrm{MS_B}}$ & \citet{bodoff_test_2007, urbano_measurement_2013} & ICC(1,$k$). It estimates the dependability index $\Phi$ of generalizability theory, not $E\rho^2$: a common trend over the window counts as error \\
\texttt{cronbach\_alpha} & $\frac{5}{4}\Big(1 - \dfrac{\sum_t \sigma^2_t}{\sigma^2_{\text{total}}}\Big)$ & \citet{cronbach_coefficient_1951} & exact. It equals $E\rho^2$ of \citet{urbano_measurement_2013} with five checkpoints as the facet \\
\texttt{eta2\_window} & $\mathrm{SS_B}/\mathrm{SS_{total}}$ & classical & exact \\
\texttt{binomial\_snr} & $\sigma_a(x_a)/\mathrm{se}_{\bar{x}}$ & \citet{card_little_2020, miller_adding_2024} & own \\
\texttt{resolved\_pairs\_binomial} & share of pairs with $d_{ab}/\sqrt{2\bar{p}_{ab}(1-\bar{p}_{ab})/n} > 1.96$ & \citet{card_little_2020, dror_hitchhikers_2018} & unpaired two-proportion test. Both sources prescribe a paired test (McNemar) for a shared test set, which needs per-item outputs. The unpaired test is conservative \\
\texttt{tie\_rate\_items} & share of pairs with $d_{ab} < 1/n$ & \citet{schaeffer_are_2023} & own: the resolution of the test set \\
\texttt{noise\_to\_binomial} & $s/\mathrm{se}_{\bar{x}}$ & \citet{madaan_quantifying_2024, wang_measuring_2025} & own. \citet{madaan_quantifying_2024} set the seed deviation beside the 95\,\% interval without forming a ratio. In the terms of \citet{wang_measuring_2025}, it is prediction over total noise \\
\texttt{mde\_resolved} & share of pairs with $d_{ab} > (1.96 + 0.84)\sqrt{2}\,s$ & \citet{card_little_2020, miller_adding_2024} & the minimum detectable effect at 80\,\% power and $\alpha = 0.05$ \citep[Eq.~10]{miller_adding_2024}, with checkpoint noise in place of the paired item-level error \\
\texttt{prob\_outperform} & $\mathrm{mean}_{(a,b)}\,\big|\hat{P}_{ab} - \tfrac12\big|$, $\hat{P}_{ab}$ the share of the $5 \times 5$ window-score pairs that $a$ wins (ties $\tfrac12$) & \citet{bouthillier_accounting_2021} & same quantity. The source pairs independent trainings, recommends at least 29, and reports whether $\hat{P} \geq 0.75$. Ours uses five consecutive checkpoints, unpaired \\
\texttt{dior} & 5th percentile, over 100 draws, of $\tau_b$ between the final ranking and a ranking that reads each variant at a random window checkpoint & \citet{perlitz_efficient_2024} & same construction: the lower confidence bound of $\tau$ between the original and a resampled ranking. The source resamples examples and scenarios (1{,}000 bootstraps). Ours resamples the checkpoint \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{Catalogue, separation: is the spread across variants large against the noise?}
\label{tab:sur-separation}
\end{table}

\texttt{kendall\_w\_window} & $W = \dfrac{12\sum_a (R_a - \bar{R})^2}{5^2 (V^3 - V)}$, $R_a$ the rank sum of variant $a$ over the window & \citet{kendall_problem_1939} & exact (no tie correction). Over tasks, $1 - W$ is the diversity of \citet{zhang_inherent_2024} \\
\texttt{sign\_consistency\_window} & share of pairs ordered the same way at all five window checkpoints & \citet{buckley_evaluating_2000} & the source's error rate is $\sum \min(|A{>}B|, |A{<}B|) / \sum$ decisions over query sets, with a 5\,\% fuzziness band. Ours is the share of pairs whose minority count is zero \\

\texttt{split\_half} & $2r/(1+r)$, $r$ the Spearman correlation between the variants' means over $\{0.80, 0.90, 1\}$ and over $\{0.85, 0.95\}$ & \citet{spearman_correlation_1910, brown_experimental_1910} & exact \\

They are circular against DA-ckpt and are not scored against it.}
