\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\scriptsize
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.215\linewidth}p{0.28\linewidth}p{0.14\linewidth}p{0.285\linewidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Statistic} & \textbf{Definition} & \textbf{Source} & \textbf{Relation to the source} \\
\midrule
\texttt{monotonicity} & $\mathrm{mean}_a\,\rho_{\text{Spearman}}(t, x_a(t))$ & \citet{kydlicek_finetasks_nodate} & exact. \citet{hofmann_fluid_2025} take $|\rho|$, \citet{yagoubi_e2lm_2025} $\max(0, \rho)$, \citet{madaan_quantifying_2024} Kendall's $\tau$ \\
\texttt{monotonicity\_steps} & $\mathrm{mean}_a\,\dfrac{\#\text{up} - \#\text{down}}{\#\text{up} + \#\text{down}}$ over consecutive tenths & \citet{heineman_signal_2025} & in the released code only (\texttt{snr/stats.py}) \\
\texttt{total\_variation} & $\mathrm{mean}_a\,\dfrac{\frac{1}{9}\sum_{t}|x_a(t{+}1) - x_a(t)|}{\max_t x_a(t) - \min_t x_a(t)}$ & \citet{heineman_signal_2025, hofmann_fluid_2025} & the released code without its improvement term. The paper's Eq.~6 subtracts $(U(T) - U(0))/T$ and has no range. \citet{hofmann_fluid_2025} divide the summed variation by $|x(T) - x(1)|$ \\
\texttt{autocorr} & $\mathrm{mean}_{a,\,\ell \in \{1,2\}}\,|r_\ell(x_a)|$, $r_\ell$ the Pearson correlation of the curve with itself at lag $\ell$ & \citet{yagoubi_e2lm_2025} & their Eqs.~3--4 with $L = \lfloor 10/4 \rfloor = 2$ lags. They centre both segments on the per-fold scoresmean of the whole curve, and we centre each segment on its own mean \\
\texttt{gain\_over\_noise} & $\mathrm{mean}_a\,(x_a(1) - x_a(0.1))/s$ & \citet{madaan_quantifying_2024} & own \\
\texttt{late\_slope\_to\_noise} & $\mathrm{mean}_a$ of the least-squares slope over $t \geq 0.5$ divided by its residual deviation & \citet{hofmann_fluid_2025} & own (saturation) \\
\texttt{nonrandom} & $q = (\bar{x} - c)/(1 - c)$ & \citet{kydlicek_finetasks_nodate} & own: the chance-corrected mean, not the criterion of Table~\ref{tab:sur-finetasks} \\
\texttt{z\_above\_chance} & $(\bar{x} - c)/\sqrt{c(1-c)/n}$ & -- & own: the one-sample binomial $z$ of our above-random gate \\
\texttt{emerged\_share} & share of variants with $x_a > c + 2\sqrt{c(1-c)/n}$ & \citet{du_understanding_2024, schaeffer_are_2023} & own. Neither source sets a statistical threshold above chance \\
\texttt{item\_information} & $q(1 - q)$ & \citet{rodriguez_evaluation_2021, vania_comparing_2021} & the Fisher information $\gamma_i^2\,p_{ij}(1 - p_{ij})$ of \citet{rodriguez_evaluation_2021} with unit discrimination, on the aggregate chance-corrected accuracy. No item response model $m$.is fitted \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{Catalogue, curve shape: does the task behave like a learnable, unsaturated measurement?}
\label{tab:sur-shape}
\end{table}

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\scriptsize
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.215\linewidth}p{0.28\linewidth}p{0.14\linewidth}p{0.285\linewidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Statistic} & \textbf{Definition} & \textbf{Source} & \textbf{Relation to the source} \\
\midrule
\texttt{prev\_rung\_da} & $\mathrm{DA}$ of the previous rung's final ranking against the proxy's & \citet{magnusson_datadecide_2025} & Eq.~\ref{eq:sur-da} one rung down \\
\texttt{ladder\_da} & mean $\mathrm{DA}$ over every pair of rungs up to the proxy & \citet{magnusson_datadecide_2025} & own \\
\texttt{rung\_sign\_consistency} & share of pairs ordered alike at every rung up to the proxy & \citet{liu_regmix_2025} & the rank-invariance hypothesis, tested pair by pair \\
\texttt{rung\_gap\_corr} & Pearson $r$ between the pair gaps $x_a - x_b$ at the previous rung and at the proxy & \citet{liu_regmix_2025} & own \\
\texttt{projected\_flip\_rate} & share of pairs whose gap, extrapolated linearly in $\log N$ from the rungs up to the proxy, changes sign at the reference's $N$ & \citet{magnusson_datadecide_2025} & own (crossovers) \\
\texttt{size\_monotonicity} & $\mathrm{mean}_a\,(\#\text{up} - \#\text{down})/(\#\text{up} + \#\text{down})$ across rungs & \citet{bhagia_establishing_2025} & own \\
\texttt{scale\_gain\_over\_noise} & $\mathrm{mean}_a\,(x_a^{\text{proxy}} - x_a^{\text{prev}})/s$ & \citet{bhagia_establishing_2025} & own \\
\texttt{pseudo\_ref\_da} & $\mathrm{DA}$ of the proxy's final ranking against the 1B rung's & \citet{magnusson_datadecide_2025, liu_regmix_2025} & reads the largest rung below the reference, not the proxy alone \\
\midrule
\texttt{bpb\_rank\_agreement} & $\mathrm{DA}$ of the task's final ranking against the ranking by bits per byte in the task's language & \citet{thrush_improving_2025} & pairwise concordance over our variants. The foldsource's estimator $\gamma_j$ weights each pair of public models by its rank gap in loss \\
\texttt{bpb\_corr\_training} & Spearman $\rho$ between the score and $-$BPB over (variant, tenth) cells & \citet{thrush_improving_2025, brandfonbrener_loss--loss_2024} & over the checkpoints of one model family, not over independent models \\
\texttt{language\_consensus} & $\mathrm{DA}$ against the mean $z$-score of the language's other benchmarks above chance & \citet{perlitz_benchbench_2024} & benchmark agreement testing, with pairwise concordance for Kendall's $\tau$, a mean $z$-score for the mean win rate, and the task left out of its reference \\
\texttt{benchmark\_consensus} & $\mathrm{DA}$ against the mean $z$-score of the same benchmark in other languages & \citet{perlitz_benchbench_2024} & as above \\
\texttt{item\_total\_corr} & Spearman $\rho$ between the final scores are obtainedand the mean $z$-score of every other benchmark above chance & \citet{ruan_observational_2024, cronbach_coefficient_1951} & the corrected item-total correlation of test theory. \citet{ruan_observational_2024} extract the common factor by partitioningPCA \\
\midrule
\texttt{n\_pairs}, \texttt{n\_items}, \texttt{chance} & $|\mathcal{P}|$, $n$, $c$ & -- & controls \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{Catalogue, the small ladder (top), agreement with the stored per-questionloss and with other benchmarks (middle), and controls.}
\label{tab:sur-agreement}
\end{table}

\paragraph{Statistics we cannot compute.}
The ladder report holds one aggregate score per checkpoint.
Statistics of per-item outputs or log-probabilities are therefore out of reach. These include the proxy metrics of \citet{magnusson_datadecide_2025} (correct probability, normalised correct probability, margin) and the transformation chain of \citet{schaeffer_why_2024}. They also include item response models \citep{polo_tinybenchmarks_2024, kipnis_metabench_2025}, paired significance tests, the almost stochastic order of \citet{dror_deep_2019} and prompt-format sensitivity \citep{alzahrani_when_2024}. Finally, they include the minimum detectable ability difference of \citet{yauney_reliable_2026}, which also needs the reference, and the token-level proxies of \citet{patel_forecasting_2026} and \citet{koh_predicting_2026}.

\paragraph{Correlations reported by the sources.}
These set the scale for our own numbers, but each is measured on a different population.
\citet{heineman_signal_2025}: SNR against decision accuracy, $R = 0.791$.
\citet{bhagia_establishing_2025}: the standard deviation of the task loss over the last ten checkpoints against the error of the accuracy prediction, $r = 0.821$.
\citet{liu_regmix_2025}: a regression fitted on 1M-parameter models predicts the ranking of data mixtures at 1B parameters with Spearman 0.97, on validation loss.
\citet{madaan_quantifying_2024}: monotonicity is 0.09 for MMLU and 0.95 for its cloze formulation.
\citet{lourie_scaling_2025} find that downstream scaling laws extrapolate in 39\,\% of cases, and \citet{wang_can_2026} that proxy rankings reverse under small changes of hyperparameters.

\subsection{How a surrogate is scored}
\label{app:sur-scoring}

Surrogates are read on the proxy only: its final checkpoint, its ten tenths, its noise window and the rungs below it.
The single evaluation run, so no inferenceexception is \texttt{pseudo\_ref\_da}.
A cell is a (task, proxy size, fraction of the run). The truth is taken over cells above chance and with at least three pairs.
For each truth and each surrogate, we report Spearman's $\rho$ over one point per (benchmark, language) cluster. Each point is the mean surrogate of the cluster against its mean truth, pooled over proxy sizes and checkpoints.
A task and its reformulated twins form one cluster, which removes the repeated measures of a task. Clusters of one benchmark still share its design (item count, answer format), so the $p$-value is a screen and not an exact test. Below 30 clusters, the $p$-value comes from a permutation test.
The Benjamini-Hochberg correction runs over every configuration of the search (truth $\times$ subset $\times$ surrogate). The only exception is the two-statistic threshold filters, which are built from the surrogates that rank highest on the same truth.
The \emph{ceiling} of a truth is its $\rho$ with itself when the reference is read at 90\,\% of its run. No surrogate can be expected to track a truth better than the truth tracks its own re-measurement.

\subsection{Summary of correlations}
\label{app:sur-results}

Tables~\ref{tab:sur-corr-catalogue} and~\ref{tab:sur-corr-snr} give the correlation of every surrogate with the three truths over the benchmark tasks, and Table~\ref{tab:sur-corr-strata} gives the correlation with DA-size within each proxy size, language tier and benchmark.
Five readings hold across them.
First, no intermediate checkpointssurrogate reaches the ceiling. The best proxy-only statistics recover $\rho \approx 0.82$--$0.83$ of a DA-size whose own re-measurement correlates at 0.92.
Second, the statistics that track decision accuracy best describe whether the proxy's ranking and curves are stable (sign persistence, consecutive Kendall's $\tau$, crossings, agreement with the rung below), not how far apart the variants are. \checknum{The dispersion signals alone (range, standard deviation, pairwise distance) carry almost nothing, with $\rho$ between $-0.02$ and $0.13$ against every truth}.
Third, the SNR of \citet{heineman_signal_2025} (relative dispersion over the relative checkpoint noise, detrended) reaches $\rho = 0.68$ with DA-size, against $R = 0.79$ in their setting. \checknum{Replacing the checkpoint noise by the $k$-fold benchmark noise raises only 6 of the 22 signals, the discrepancy and depth ones}.
Fourth, the benchmark noise is largely a function of the item count, and the item count alone correlates at 0.37, \checknum{about half the best ratio (0.79)}. \texttt{dispersion\_shifted} is constant, so its ratios are the inverse noise.
Fifth, the pooled correlation hides large differences between strata (Table~\ref{tab:sur-corr-strata}). The relative-standard-deviation SNR reaches $\rho = 0.79$ over all clusters but 0.51--0.67 within one proxy size, 0.81 within HellaSwag and 0.27 on the per-language bits per byte.

On our ladder, we take the residual standard deviation around a least-squares line through the five checkpoints of the last 20\% of training (three degrees of freedom), since scores still rise over the learning-rate decay, and keep this raw standard deviation as a variant.

\textbf{Benchmark noise.} We propose to measure noise as the relative standard deviation of scores across $k = 5$ disjoint folds of the evaluation set, averaged across models:

$$\text{Noise}(M) = \frac{1}{|M|} \sum_{m \in M} \frac{\sqrt{\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=1}^{k}(m_i - \bar{m})^2}}{\bar{m}}$$

where $m_1, \dots, m_k$ are the per-fold scores of model $m$. We adoptconsider the benchmark noise overbeside the original checkpoint-to-checkpoint noise for two reasons. First, it is computable from a single evaluation run, since fold scores are derived by partitioning stored per-question outputs without rerunning inference. This makes the framework applicable to any public model, including those without released intermediate checkpoints. Second, on its own it correlates slightly more strongly with decision accuracy than checkpoint noise ($R($\rho = 0.808$ vs. $R-0.46$ vs.\ $\rho = 0.760$).-0.44$ with DA-size). As the denominator of the SNR, however, the detrended checkpoint noise does better for 16 of the 22 signals.

\section{Surrogates of Decision Accuracy: Definitions}
\label{app:surrogates}

A \emph{surrogate} is a statistic of a benchmark that is computed on the proxy size alone andalone. It is meant to tell, without training the reference, whether theproxy's ranking of the proxy can be trusted.
This appendix defines every surrogate we compute:compute. These are the signal-to-noise ratio of \citet{heineman_signal_2025} with all its signal and noise variants (\S\ref{app:sur-snr}--\ref{app:sur-noise}), the task-selection criteria of FineTasks (\S\ref{app:sur-finetasks}), and a catalogue of statistics from theevaluation-reliability literature on evaluation reliability (\S\ref{app:sur-catalogue}).
For each we give the formula as implemented, the paper it comes from, and, where the two differ, the difference.
\S\ref{app:sur-scoring} describes how surrogates are scored and \S\ref{app:sur-results} gives the summary table of correlations.

A task is scored on the $V$ design variants of one proxy size.
Variant $a$ has a training curve $x_a(t)$ at the ten evaluated tenths $t \in \{0.1,\dots,1\}$ of its run, a final score $x_a = x_a(1)$, and a \emph{noise window} $W_a = \{x_a(t) : t \in \{0.80, 0.85, 0.90, 0.95, 1\}\}$ with mean $\bar{w}_a$ and checkpoint noise $s_a$, the residual standard deviation $s_a$.of $W_a$ around a least-squares line through its five points (three degrees of freedom). \emph{Differences from the source.}
\citet{magnusson_datadecide_2025} take the mean of three seeds as the target and average the decision accuracy of three single-seed proxies; ourproxies. Our grid has one seed per variant on both sides.
Neither paper treats ties, and \citet{heineman_signal_2025} derive $\tau = 2\,\mathrm{DA} - 1$ under that assumption.
Under Eq.~\ref{eq:sur-da}Eq.~\ref{eq:sur-da}, a pair tied on both sides agrees and a pair tied on one side is a miss, somiss. So with $C$ concordant, $D$ discordant, $T_2$ doubly tied and $T_1$ singly tied pairs, $2\,\mathrm{DA} - 1 = \tau_a + (T_2 - T_1)/|\mathcal{P}|$.

\begin{equation}

@@ -61,14 +64,14 @@ With $s^{0}_a$ the population (divide by $n_c$) standard deviation of $W_a$:
(i) The signal is divided by the mean of the window means, not by the mean of the final scores.
(ii) The noise is a ratio of means over models, not a mean of per-model ratios.
(iii) The standard deviation divides by $n_c$, not $n_c - 1$, which makes the noise smaller by $\sqrt{4/5}$ at $n_c = 5$ and the SNR larger by 1.118.
Our window is five checkpoints spread over the last 20\,\% of the run, where the original uses the last five saved checkpoints. Our noise replaces $s^{0}_a$ in Eq.~\ref{eq:sur-snr-code} by the detrended $s_a$, and the raw $s^{0}_a$ remains as a variant.

\begin{equation}

\citet{heineman_signal_2025} define three further noises that validate the checkpoint noise and do not enter the grid.
\emph{Seed noise} is $\sigma(M)$ over runs that differ in the seed, with $M_i = \frac{1}{n_c}\sum_{j=T-n_c+1}^{T} U(t_j)$; \emph{data-orderU(t_j)$. \emph{Data-order noise} is the same over runs that differ in the order of documents.

They report $R^2$ of 0.82, 0.86 and 0.95 between these and the checkpoint noise.
On our ladder the seed noise is the sample standard deviation of the final score over the three seeds of the replicated cells;cells. We use itis used to size effects, not as a surrogate.
In the terms of \citet{wang_measuring_2025}, checkpoint and seed noise are prediction noise, the $k$-fold noise is data noise, and a training curve never shows the latter because it re-evaluates the same items.

\subsection{Summary of correlations}
\label{app:sur-results}

Tables~\ref{tab:sur-corr-catalogue} and~\ref{tab:sur-corr-snr} give the correlation of every surrogate with the three truths over the benchmark tasks.
Fourtasks, and Table~\ref{tab:sur-corr-strata} gives the correlation with DA-size within each proxy size, language tier and benchmark.
Five readings hold across them.
First, no surrogate reaches the ceiling: theceiling. The best proxy-only statistics recover $\rho \approx 0.73$--$0.76$0.82$--$0.83$ of a DA-size whose own re-measurement correlates at 0.90.0.92.
Second, the statistics that track decision accuracy best describe whether the proxy's ranking and curves are stable (sign persistence, autocorrelation, monotonicity, Cronbach's $\alpha$),consecutive Kendall's $\tau$, crossings, agreement with the rung below), not how far apart the variants are: \checknum{theare. \checknum{The dispersion signals alone (range, standard deviation, pairwise distance) correlate negativelycarry almost nothing, with $\rho$ between $-0.02$ and $0.13$ against every truth}.
Third, the SNR of \citet{heineman_signal_2025} as released(relative dispersion over the relative checkpoint noise, detrended) reaches $\rho = 0.35$0.68$ with DA-size, against $R = 0.79$ in their setting; \checknum{replacingsetting. \checknum{Replacing the checkpoint noise by the $k$-fold benchmark noise raises every signal}.only 6 of the 22 signals, the discrepancy and depth ones}.
Fourth, the benchmark noise is largely a function of the item count, and the item count alone correlates at 0.38, \checknum{as much as0.37, \checknum{about half the best ratio}:ratio (0.79)}. \texttt{dispersion\_shifted} is constant, so its ratios are the inverse noise.
Fifth, the pooled correlation hides large differences between strata (Table~\ref{tab:sur-corr-strata}). The relative-standard-deviation SNR reaches $\rho = 0.79$ over all clusters but 0.51--0.67 within one proxy size, 0.81 within HellaSwag and 0.27 on the per-language bits per byte.

\newpage

\subsection*{Model Card for the Multilingual Signal-and-Noise Model Ladder}

A ladder of \nruns decoder-only language models (90M--3B non-embedding parameters), pretrained from scratch on controlled multilingual data mixtures. We release them with all intermediate checkpoints, to study which evaluation benchmarks give reliable signal at small scale.

\subsection{The above-chance gate}

\paragraph{Question.} Which\noindent\begin{minipage}[t]{0.485\textwidth}
\vspace{0pt}
\paragraph{Research Question.} \emph{Which benchmarks clear chance at which size of the ladder, and which separate the language settings most?most?}

\paragraph{Setup.} Seed-1904 runs of every language setting, ladder and data build, 90M--1.7B. A run clears chance on a task when the one-sided 95\% Wilson lower bound of its accuracy over the task's items is above the chance level. A (task, size) cell is above chance when at least half of the runs of that size that train the task's language clear it. Tasks without a chance level (bits per byte) are not gated.
\end{minipage}\hfill
\begin{minipage}[t]{0.485\textwidth}
\vspace{0pt}

The gate removes most of the evaluation somewhere on the ladder.} On the 841 trained tasks, 312311 (37 \%) are above chance at 90M and 489 (58507 (60 \%) at 1.7B. 550547 (65 \%) are at chance at one size or more, 326 (39313 (37 \%) at every size. (Figure~\ref{fig:app_rq00_gate_and_curves}.)
2. \textbf{Over\\
\\
\textbf{2. Over all 1,3731,679 chance-level tasks the picture is the same.} 552565 of 1,372 (401,433 scored (39 \%) pass at 90M and 778954 of 1,3731,663 (57 \%) at 1.7B. 552 (40682 (41 \%) never pass.

3. \textbf{Itpass, 16 of them never scored.
\\

\textbf{3. It is an answer-format effect, not an option-count one.} On the trained languages at 1.7B the letter-answer families nearly vanish while their cloze twins pass: \texttt{belebele} 3 / 59 tasks against 57 / 59, \texttt{global\_mmlu\_full} 1 / 29 against 29 / 29, \texttt{include\_base\_44} 3 / 36 against 29 / 36.

\paragraph{Question.} How\noindent\begin{minipage}[t]{0.485\textwidth}

\\
\\
\textbf{2. Fewer than half the items are kept.} They are 45 \% of the items of the 507 tasks the committed gate passes at 1.7B, and 44 \% over all 840 tasks with an item above chance.
\\
\\
\textbf{3. Recomputed on the kept items, the gate passes 826almost every task.} It passes 816 of those 840 tasks at 1.7B and 82--9083--90 \% of the 841 at the proxies, against 37 \% (90M) to 5860 \% (1.7B) for the full benchmarks: the in-sample selection all but guarantees the reference's verdict and lifts every proxy's.
\end{rqfinding}
\end{minipage}

\paragraph{Question.} If every benchmark-language task keeps only the items its 1.7B runs answer above chance, how much do decision accuracy, SNR, the above-random gate and the scaling fit improve, and does it matter whether the gate is applied before or after the items are chosen?

\paragraph{Setup.} Seed-1904 runs of every cell, 90M--1.7B, final checkpoints. Every benchmark-language task keeps only the items that its 1.7B runs answer above chance, after the above-chance gate. We compare it with the full task. The selection reads the reference by design, so the gains are an upper bound, not a held-out estimate.

\section{English-only\twocolumn[{%
\subsection{English-only models}
\label{app:rq13_english_only}

\begin{rqfinding}
1. \textbf{The English-only cells are better at English, by a small margin.} Deep K1 wins more than half of the English accuracy tasks against every other K at every drawn size (25 of 25 (size, K) cells, win share 0.56--0.79 per size, every K pooled), but the mean gap at 1.7B is 1.2 points (33 tasks, every K pooled). Shallow K1 wins 28 of 30 cells, 0.6 points at 1.7B. (Figure~\ref{fig:app_rq13_english_only}.)

2. \textbf{English BPB is the cleanest separation.} K1 has the lower \texttt{bpb\_dclm} in all 30 (size, K) comparisons at both depths. The deep gap is 0.049 bits per byte at 90M and shrinks to 0.028 at 1.7B (0.782 against 0.810).

3. \textbf{Part of the lead is beyond seed noise.} Deep K1 is ahead by more than 2 seed sd in 0.55 (175M), 0.45 (600M) and 0.45 (1B) of the (task, K) comparisons and behind by as much in 0.03--0.07.
\end{rqfinding}

If the trainings are correct they should do clearly better on the (English) benchmarks than the multilingual cells of the same size: higher scores, above chance earlier, and at least as predictable in size, as reliable in their decisions and as high in SNR. Does the ladder show that, and do the K1 numbers land where AllenAI's DataDecide puts the same English tasks?tasks?}

These other cells give half of their tokens to English. We use seed 1904, final checkpoints and sizes 90M--1.7B, on the English accuracy tasks above chance at each size.
